Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doctor... I guess what brings me in is my head just feels so cloudy lately. Like, I'll be sitting at my desk staring at a board packet for the condo association, or looking at an email from one of our vendors, and I just... I cannot decide on even the simple things. Things I used to handle real fast, you know? 

It's just a fog. Like just the other day, I was looking at a message from the landscaping company, this guy Tony, he runs it. He's been doing our lawns for, oh, maybe fifteen years? Back when my husband was still around, Tony used to come by and they'd talk about classic cars for hours out in the driveway. We had a 1968 Mustang. Beautiful cherry red, white interior. Kept it pristine, never took it out in the rain. 

But anyway, Tony sent this email about the spring mulch order, and I just sat there staring at the screen for I don't know how long. I couldn't even figure out what to tell him, and usually I'd just know, snap, just like that.

Depressed? I don't... I wouldn't say depressed, exactly. Just that fog I told you about. I mean, I guess I felt a little sad the other day, but that was just because Mrs. Gable's dog got sick. She lives over in building C. It's a little Shih Tzu, you know, the ones with the squished faces? She brought him right into the association office to tell me.

Because this awful man who used to rent unit 4B—he moved to Florida, thank goodness—but he used to let his pet ferret run loose in the billiards room while he played pool. And let me tell you, ferrets have a terrible smell. Just awful. It took us three weeks and a professional carpet cleaner to get the musk out of those rugs, and we had to assess him for the bill, which was a whole other headache because he tried to say it was the plumbing. 

But anyway, I'm sorry, what were you asking me about?

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, goodness, no. My husband passed away almost seven years ago now. Bless his heart, it was a sudden thing. But no, this fog... this is recent. Just the last few weeks, really. 

Though speaking of my husband, he used to handle all the big decisions around the house. He was an engineer, you see. Worked for the city water department for thirty-five years. He had this whole system for organizing his tools in the garage. Pegboards, little labels made from that label maker we bought at Sears... remember Sears? We used to go to the one at the mall on Saturdays. They had a wonderful hardware section, and right next to it was the candy counter where they sold those warm cashews. We'd always get a quarter pound of cashews before we looked at the lawnmowers. 

The mall is practically empty now, though. Just a bunch of cell phone stores and a place that sells those big massage chairs. I went in there last month to look for a birthday gift for my niece, Sarah—she just turned 40, can you believe it?—and it was just so depressing. 

But no, this... this trouble deciding things, it didn't start back when he died. I handled everything just fine for years. It's just lately that my brain feels like it's full of cotton wool.

My husband passed away almost seven years ago now. Bless his heart, it was a sudden thing. But no, this fog... this is recent. Just the last few weeks, really. \n\nThough speaking of my husband, he used to handle all the big decisions around the house. He was an engineer, you see. Worked for the city water department for thirty-five years. He had this whole system for organizing his tools in the garage. Pegboards, little labels made from that label maker we bought at Sears... remember Sears? We used to go to the one at the mall on Saturdays. They had a wonderful hardware section, and right next to it was the candy counter where they sold those warm cashews. We'd always get a quarter pound of cashews before we looked at the lawnmowers. \n\nThe mall is practically empty now, though. Just a bunch of cell phone stores and a place that sells those big massage chairs. I went in there last month to look for a birthday gift for my niece, Sarah—she just turned 40, can you believe it?—and it was just so depressing. \n\nBut no, this... this trouble deciding things, it didn't start back when he died. Worried? Well, I don't know if I'd say worried... I mean, I guess I've been a little anxious about the new recycling bins they want to put in at the complex. The city just sent out this huge packet about separating the glass from the plastics, and they want us to use these big blue carts.

Forgetting things? No, I don't think my memory is any worse than it usually is. I mean, I still remember all the bylaws for the association, and there are over forty pages of them.

Kept it pristine, never took it out in the rain. \n\nBut anyway, Tony sent this email about the spring mulch order, and I just sat there staring at the screen for I don't know how long. I couldn't even figure out what to tell him, and usually I'd just know, snap, just like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Depressed? I don't... My husband passed away almost seven years ago now. Bless his heart, it was a sudden thing. But no, this fog... this is recent. Just the last few weeks, really. \n\nThough speaking of my husband, he used to handle all the big decisions around the house. He was an engineer, you see. Worked for the city water department for thirty-five years. He had this whole system for organizing his tools in the garage. Pegboards, little labels made from that label maker we bought at Sears... remember Sears? We used to go to the one at the mall on Saturdays. They had a wonderful hardware section, and right next to it was the candy counter where they sold those warm cashews. We'd always get a quarter pound of cashews before we looked at the lawnmowers. \n\nThe mall is practically empty now, though. Just a bunch of cell phone stores and a place that sells those big massage chairs. I went in there last month to look for a birthday gift for my niece, Sarah—she just turned 40, can you believe it?—and it was just so depressing. \n\nBut no, this... this trouble deciding things, it didn't start back when he died. It's just lately that my brain feels like it's full of cotton wool.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually worried or anxious lately?\n\nPatient: Worried? Well, I don't know if I'd say worried... I was telling my neighbor, Helen—she's in 2A, right above the boiler room—I said, \"Helen, how are the older folks supposed to drag those things all the way to the curb?\" Helen has terrible arthritis in her knees. She had a replacement back in 2018, Dr. Vance did it over at Memorial. He's a wonderful surgeon, very handsome man, looks a bit like that actor... oh, what's his name? The one who was in that movie with the boat? \n\nAnyway, Helen was saying she might have to hire her grandson to come take the bins out. Her grandson is a lovely boy, goes to the community college, studying to be a dental hygienist. Which I think is a very smart career choice, people always need their teeth cleaned. I go to Dr. Miller myself, he's got an office over by the old post office. The one they closed down last year because of the mold in the ceiling? Such a shame, it was a beautiful historic building. \n\nBut I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, being worried. No, I wouldn't say I'm unusually worried about anything in particular, just the usual things, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you been forgetting recent things more than usual?\n\nPatient: Forgetting things? Today's date? Oh, let's see, it's... well, it's Tuesday, I know that much because the street sweeper always comes on Tuesdays. They just changed the schedule for that, actually. They used to come on Thursdays, but the city council had a big meeting about it last month. He went down to city hall to complain because the new sweeper truck is so loud it wakes up his terrier, Barnaby. 

Barnaby is a rescue, sweetest little dog, but he has terrible anxiety. Mr. Henderson bought him one of those thunder shirts, you know, the ones that wrap real tight? I don't know if it works, but I told him he should try playing classical music. That's what I did for my cat, Mr. Whiskers, God rest his soul. He loved Chopin. Especially the nocturnes. I had this lovely CD player I bought at Circuit City... oh, remember Circuit City? With the red plug outside? 

But anyway, the date... well, I know it's October. Or is it November already? The leaves on the big oak out front are just starting to turn that pretty rust color. So I suppose I don't know the exact number, but it's Tuesday in the fall.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Sick? Oh, no, I've been feeling pretty good physically. Just the usual aches and pains, you know. Actually, speaking of being sick, did you hear about the terrible flu going around the elementary school down the street? 

Mrs. Gable—she's the crossing guard, lovely lady, always wears that bright yellow vest with the reflective stripes—she was telling me that half the third grade was out last week. Her daughter is one of the teachers there. She teaches art, which I think is just so wonderful. Children need a creative outlet, don't you think? I used to love art class when I was a girl. We made these little ashtrays out of clay for Mother's Day. Nobody smokes anymore, of course, which is for the best. My uncle Arthur smoked two packs a day of those unfiltered cigarettes. He had this awful cough, sounded like a tractor trying to start in the winter. He drove a tractor, actually, had a farm out in the valley... grew the most amazing sweet corn. We'd have corn on the cob every Sunday in the summer, with lots of butter. 

But no, to answer your question, I haven't caught any of those bugs going around. I make sure to wash my hands very thoroughly, especially after getting the mail. You never know who's been touching the envelopes.

I know because my neighbor, Mr. Henderson—he's the one with that beautiful rose garden, you know, the really big yellow ones that smell like peaches? Just the usual aches and pains, you know. Actually, speaking of being sick, did you hear about the terrible flu going around the elementary school down the street? \n\nMrs. Gable—she's the crossing guard, lovely lady, always wears that bright yellow vest with the reflective stripes—she was telling me that half the third grade was out last week. Her daughter is one of the teachers there. She teaches art, which I think is just so wonderful. Children need a creative outlet, don't you think? I used to love art class when I was a girl. We made these little ashtrays out of clay for Mother's Day. Nobody smokes anymore, of course, which is for the best. My uncle Arthur smoked two packs a day of those unfiltered cigarettes. He had this awful cough, sounded like a tractor trying to start in the winter. He drove a tractor, actually, had a farm out in the valley... grew the most amazing sweet corn. We'd have corn on the cob every Sunday in the summer, with lots of butter. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I haven't caught any of those bugs going around. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Confused about where I am? Oh, heavens no. I know exactly where I am, I've got a very good sense of direction. Always have. 

My husband, Richard—bless his heart—he couldn't navigate his way out of a paper bag. We took a trip to Florida back in '98, drove the whole way. We were supposed to be going to Daytona, but he missed the exit and wouldn't stop to ask for directions. Men are like that, aren't they? So stubborn. We ended up at this little diner off the highway, and they had the most fantastic key lime pie. Real key limes, not that bright green artificial stuff you get at the grocery store. The waitress told us her grandmother made it fresh every morning. She was a sweet girl, studying to be a nurse. I always thought nursing was such a noble profession. You have to have a real calling for it, taking care of sick people all day. I used to volunteer at the hospital gift shop, arranging the flowers. People love getting flowers when they're not feeling well. Carnations last the longest, but roses are just so elegant...

What were we talking about? Oh, getting lost! No, no, I never get confused about where I am. My internal compass works just fine.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Sad? Well, no, not really. I mean, I have my moments, but who doesn't at my age? Mostly I just keep busy. Keeping busy is the secret, you know. 

I was just telling my niece that the other day. She just got her real estate license, which I think is wonderful, but she gets so stressed out. I told her, "Honey, you just have to keep moving." When I managed the Oakwood Condominiums—oh, that was years ago now, before they put in that new pool—I never stopped moving. We had 120 units, and there was always something. A leaky faucet, a noisy neighbor... Mrs. Higgins in 4B was always complaining about the people upstairs walking too loud. But they were just normal walkers! They had this beautiful Persian rug, though, to try and muffle the sound. Deep red with gold threading. I always wanted a rug like that, but with my allergies, hardwood floors are just so much easier to keep clean. You just run a dust mop over them and you're done. Have you tried those microfiber ones? They pick up everything. Dog hair, dust bunnies... 

But anyway, no, I wouldn't say I've been feeling sad. Just... busy.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Trouble concentrating? Well, actually, yes. That's a big part of what brings me in. My head just feels so... cloudy lately. I'll sit down and stare at a board packet, or look at a vendor email, and I just cannot decide on even the simple things I used to handle real fast. 

It's so frustrating. Like the other day, I was looking at an email from the new landscaping vendor—we had to switch companies, you know, because the old one kept leaving grass clippings all over the walkways. You just can't have that. People track it right into the lobby, and those lobby carpets cost an absolute fortune to steam clean. We use this company from over in Westville for the carpets, and the young man who comes out, he's just the sweetest thing. Always takes his shoes off at the door. He reminds me so much of my nephew, David. 

David is so tall, he plays basketball for his high school. He's hoping to get a scholarship somewhere warm, like Arizona or Texas. I went to Arizona once, back in the eighties. The heat is different there, they call it a dry heat, but hot is hot if you ask me. We bought these beautiful turquoise necklaces from a little roadside stand near the canyon. The woman selling them made them all by hand. It must take so much patience to string all those tiny little beads together. I don't have the eyes for that kind of close-up work anymore. I have to wear my reading glasses just to look at the newspaper. 

But yes, my head just gets all foggy when I try to work on the property files. I just sit there staring at the screen.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF06.7, G31.84, F32.9, F33.9, F41.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in today. What brings you in?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor... I guess what brings me in is my head just feels so cloudy lately. Like, I'll be sitting at my desk staring at a board packet for the condo association, or looking at an email from one of our vendors, and I just... I cannot decide on even the simple things. Things I used to handle real fast, you know? \n\nIt's just a fog. Like just the other day, I was looking at a message from the landscaping company, this guy Tony, he runs it. He's been doing our lawns for, oh, maybe fifteen years? Back when my husband was still around, Tony used to come by and they'd talk about classic cars for hours out in the driveway. We had a 1968 Mustang. Beautiful cherry red, white interior. Kept it pristine, never took it out in the rain. \n\nBut anyway, Tony sent this email about the spring mulch order, and I just sat there staring at the screen for I don't know how long. I couldn't even figure out what to tell him, and usually I'd just know, snap, just like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Depressed? I don't... I wouldn't say depressed, exactly. Just that fog I told you about. I mean, I guess I felt a little sad the other day, but that was just because Mrs. Gable's dog got sick. She lives over in building C. It's a little Shih Tzu, you know, the ones with the squished faces? She brought him right into the association office to tell me. \n\nNow, technically, we have a very strict no-pets-in-the-clubhouse rule. I had to put that rule in myself back in, oh, must have been 2012. Because this awful man who used to rent unit 4B—he moved to Florida, thank goodness—but he used to let his pet ferret run loose in the billiards room while he played pool. And let me tell you, ferrets have a terrible smell. Just awful. It took us three weeks and a professional carpet cleaner to get the musk out of those rugs, and we had to assess him for the bill, which was a whole other headache because he tried to say it was the plumbing. \n\nBut anyway, I'm sorry, what were you asking me about?\n\nDoctor: Did these problems start after your husband died?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. My husband passed away almost seven years ago now. Bless his heart, it was a sudden thing. But no, this fog... this is recent. Just the last few weeks, really. \n\nThough speaking of my husband, he used to handle all the big decisions around the house. He was an engineer, you see. Worked for the city water department for thirty-five years. He had this whole system for organizing his tools in the garage. Pegboards, little labels made from that label maker we bought at Sears... remember Sears? We used to go to the one at the mall on Saturdays. They had a wonderful hardware section, and right next to it was the candy counter where they sold those warm cashews. We'd always get a quarter pound of cashews before we looked at the lawnmowers. \n\nThe mall is practically empty now, though. Just a bunch of cell phone stores and a place that sells those big massage chairs. I went in there last month to look for a birthday gift for my niece, Sarah—she just turned 40, can you believe it?—and it was just so depressing. \n\nBut no, this... this trouble deciding things, it didn't start back when he died. I handled everything just fine for years. It's just lately that my brain feels like it's full of cotton wool.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually worried or anxious lately?\n\nPatient: Worried? Well, I don't know if I'd say worried... I mean, I guess I've been a little anxious about the new recycling bins they want to put in at the complex. The city just sent out this huge packet about separating the glass from the plastics, and they want us to use these big blue carts. \n\nNow, I don't know if you've seen these new carts, but they are enormous. I was telling my neighbor, Helen—she's in 2A, right above the boiler room—I said, \"Helen, how are the older folks supposed to drag those things all the way to the curb?\" Helen has terrible arthritis in her knees. She had a replacement back in 2018, Dr. Vance did it over at Memorial. He's a wonderful surgeon, very handsome man, looks a bit like that actor... oh, what's his name? The one who was in that movie with the boat? \n\nAnyway, Helen was saying she might have to hire her grandson to come take the bins out. Her grandson is a lovely boy, goes to the community college, studying to be a dental hygienist. Which I think is a very smart career choice, people always need their teeth cleaned. I go to Dr. Miller myself, he's got an office over by the old post office. The one they closed down last year because of the mold in the ceiling? Such a shame, it was a beautiful historic building. \n\nBut I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, being worried. No, I wouldn't say I'm unusually worried about anything in particular, just the usual things, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you been forgetting recent things more than usual?\n\nPatient: Forgetting things? No, I don't think my memory is any worse than it usually is. I mean, I still remember all the bylaws for the association, and there are over forty pages of them. \n\nThough, speaking of memory, I was trying to remember the name of that wonderful little bakery that used to be on 5th Street. They made the most incredible lemon bars. My sister Margaret used to buy them every time we had a family picnic. Margaret lives up in Seattle now, it rains so much there. I don't know how she stands it, all that damp. It's terrible for your joints. I sent her a lovely umbrella for Christmas, one of those clear dome ones so you can still see where you're walking. The mail carrier, Dave, he said those are the best kind. Dave is such a nice man, he always makes sure to put my packages right inside the screen door so they don't get wet. He's retiring next year, going to buy an RV and drive to all the national parks. I told him he should start with Yellowstone, I went there when I was a little girl... \n\nOh, dear, I'm wandering again, aren't I? What was the question? Memory? No, my memory is just fine, thank you.\n\nDoctor: What is today’s date?\n\nPatient: Today's date? Oh, let's see, it's... well, it's Tuesday, I know that much because the street sweeper always comes on Tuesdays. They just changed the schedule for that, actually. They used to come on Thursdays, but the city council had a big meeting about it last month. I know because my neighbor, Mr. Henderson—he's the one with that beautiful rose garden, you know, the really big yellow ones that smell like peaches? He went down to city hall to complain because the new sweeper truck is so loud it wakes up his terrier, Barnaby. \n\nBarnaby is a rescue, sweetest little dog, but he has terrible anxiety. Mr. Henderson bought him one of those thunder shirts, you know, the ones that wrap real tight? I don't know if it works, but I told him he should try playing classical music. That's what I did for my cat, Mr. Whiskers, God rest his soul. He loved Chopin. Especially the nocturnes. I had this lovely CD player I bought at Circuit City... oh, remember Circuit City? With the red plug outside? \n\nBut anyway, the date... well, I know it's October. Or is it November already? The leaves on the big oak out front are just starting to turn that pretty rust color. So I suppose I don't know the exact number, but it's Tuesday in the fall.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sick lately?\n\nPatient: Sick? Oh, no, I've been feeling pretty good physically. Just the usual aches and pains, you know. Actually, speaking of being sick, did you hear about the terrible flu going around the elementary school down the street? \n\nMrs. Gable—she's the crossing guard, lovely lady, always wears that bright yellow vest with the reflective stripes—she was telling me that half the third grade was out last week. Her daughter is one of the teachers there. She teaches art, which I think is just so wonderful. Children need a creative outlet, don't you think? I used to love art class when I was a girl. We made these little ashtrays out of clay for Mother's Day. Nobody smokes anymore, of course, which is for the best. My uncle Arthur smoked two packs a day of those unfiltered cigarettes. He had this awful cough, sounded like a tractor trying to start in the winter. He drove a tractor, actually, had a farm out in the valley... grew the most amazing sweet corn. We'd have corn on the cob every Sunday in the summer, with lots of butter. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I haven't caught any of those bugs going around. I make sure to wash my hands very thoroughly, especially after getting the mail. You never know who's been touching the envelopes.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times lately when you felt confused about where you were?\n\nPatient: Confused about where I am? Oh, heavens no. I know exactly where I am, I've got a very good sense of direction. Always have. \n\nMy husband, Richard—bless his heart—he couldn't navigate his way out of a paper bag. We took a trip to Florida back in '98, drove the whole way. We were supposed to be going to Daytona, but he missed the exit and wouldn't stop to ask for directions. Men are like that, aren't they? So stubborn. We ended up at this little diner off the highway, and they had the most fantastic key lime pie. Real key limes, not that bright green artificial stuff you get at the grocery store. The waitress told us her grandmother made it fresh every morning. She was a sweet girl, studying to be a nurse. I always thought nursing was such a noble profession. You have to have a real calling for it, taking care of sick people all day. I used to volunteer at the hospital gift shop, arranging the flowers. People love getting flowers when they're not feeling well. Carnations last the longest, but roses are just so elegant...\n\nWhat were we talking about? Oh, getting lost! No, no, I never get confused about where I am. My internal compass works just fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most days lately?\n\nPatient: Sad? Well, no, not really. I mean, I have my moments, but who doesn't at my age? Mostly I just keep busy. Keeping busy is the secret, you know. \n\nI was just telling my niece that the other day. She just got her real estate license, which I think is wonderful, but she gets so stressed out. I told her, \"Honey, you just have to keep moving.\" When I managed the Oakwood Condominiums—oh, that was years ago now, before they put in that new pool—I never stopped moving. We had 120 units, and there was always something. A leaky faucet, a noisy neighbor... Mrs. Higgins in 4B was always complaining about the people upstairs walking too loud. But they were just normal walkers! They had this beautiful Persian rug, though, to try and muffle the sound. Deep red with gold threading. I always wanted a rug like that, but with my allergies, hardwood floors are just so much easier to keep clean. You just run a dust mop over them and you're done. Have you tried those microfiber ones? They pick up everything. Dog hair, dust bunnies... \n\nBut anyway, no, I wouldn't say I've been feeling sad. Just... busy.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Trouble concentrating? Well, actually, yes. That's a big part of what brings me in. My head just feels so... cloudy lately. I'll sit down and stare at a board packet, or look at a vendor email, and I just cannot decide on even the simple things I used to handle real fast. \n\nIt's so frustrating. Like the other day, I was looking at an email from the new landscaping vendor—we had to switch companies, you know, because the old one kept leaving grass clippings all over the walkways. You just can't have that. People track it right into the lobby, and those lobby carpets cost an absolute fortune to steam clean. We use this company from over in Westville for the carpets, and the young man who comes out, he's just the sweetest thing. Always takes his shoes off at the door. He reminds me so much of my nephew, David. \n\nDavid is so tall, he plays basketball for his high school. He's hoping to get a scholarship somewhere warm, like Arizona or Texas. I went to Arizona once, back in the eighties. The heat is different there, they call it a dry heat, but hot is hot if you ask me. We bought these beautiful turquoise necklaces from a little roadside stand near the canyon. The woman selling them made them all by hand. It must take so much patience to string all those tiny little beads together. I don't have the eyes for that kind of close-up work anymore. I have to wear my reading glasses just to look at the newspaper. \n\nBut yes, my head just gets all foggy when I try to work on the property files. I just sit there staring at the screen.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Bipolar II Disorder. You are about\nto talk with the doctor.\n\nLooking at the dialogue so far and the doctor's last question, work out what it is asking\nabout, and pick the item of your symptom profile to answer it with.\n\n[Symptom Profiles]\n- key: S031\n  name: Diminished_Thinking\n  description: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.\n  manifestation: What brings me in is that my head feels cloudy, like I stare at a board packet or a vendor email and cannot decide even simple things I used to handle fast.\n- key: S025\n  name: Loss_of_Interest\n  description: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.\n  manifestation: I have not wanted to go to my dance class, look at the little galleries, or cook Sunday food for my family, and those are things that usually make me feel like myself.\n- key: S026\n  name: Weight_Appetite_Change\n  description: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.\n  manifestation: My eating has been off, where coffee and a few bites carry me most of the day and then at night I pick at tortillas or sweets without really enjoying them.\n- key: S028\n  name: Psychomotor_Changes\n  description: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.\n  manifestation: At home I move slow like my legs are heavy, but in meetings I catch myself tapping my pen, shifting in the chair, and feeling too keyed up to sit still.\n- key: S030\n  name: Worthlessness_Guilt\n  description: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).\n  manifestation: I keep thinking I let the residents down by speaking too big and promising things before checking the money, even though nobody has said I ruined anything.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: A few mornings I have had the scary thought that maybe it would be easier if I just did not wake up, but I do not want to hurt myself and I have no plan.\n- key: S033\n  name: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\n  description: A distinct So stubborn. We ended up at this little diner off the highway, and they had the most fantastic key lime pie. Real key limes, not that bright green artificial stuff you get at the grocery store. We use this company from over in Westville for the carpets, and the young man who comes out, he's just the sweetest thing. Always takes his shoes off at the door. He reminds me so much of my nephew, David. \n\nDavid is so tall, he plays basketball for his high school. He's hoping to get a scholarship somewhere warm, like Arizona or Texas. I went to Arizona once, back in the eighties. The heat is different there, they call it a dry heat, but hot is hot if you ask me. We bought these beautiful turquoise necklaces from a little roadside stand near the canyon. The woman selling them made them all by hand. It must take so much patience to string all those tiny little beads together. I don't have the eyes for that kind of close-up work anymore. I have to wear my reading glasses just to look at the newspaper. \n\nBut yes, my head just gets all foggy when I try to work on the property files. All day? Oh, well... I'm not really sure, honestly. I haven't paid that close attention to the clock. Sometimes it feels like it's just always there, hovering over me, you know? 

Like a thick fog rolling in off the lake. We used to have a cabin up on Lake Winnipesaukee when the kids were little. The fog there in the early mornings was just incredible. You couldn't see ten feet in front of the dock. My husband would take the boys out fishing in that little aluminum rowboat we had. I was always so terrified they'd get run over by one of those big speedboats, but they loved it. Caught some beautiful trout out there. I used to pan-fry them with just a little butter and lemon. You don't need all those fancy seasonings when the fish is fresh. The grocery store fish just doesn't taste the same, does it? It always smells a little off, no matter how much you rinse it. I tried buying salmon last week, but it was just so expensive. Everything is expensive now. A gallon of milk costs a small fortune. I remember when you could get a whole cart of groceries for twenty dollars. 

But, um... no, I haven't really noticed if the fogginess changes during the day. It just feels like my brain is moving through molasses whenever I try to do my paperwork.

My husband would take the boys out fishing in that little aluminum rowboat we had. I was always so terrified they'd get run over by one of those big speedboats, but they loved it. Caught some beautiful trout out there. I used to pan-fry them with just a little butter and lemon. You don't need all those fancy seasonings when the fish is fresh. The grocery store fish just doesn't taste the same, does it? It always smells a little off, no matter how much you rinse it. I tried buying salmon last week, but it was just so expensive. Everything is expensive now. A gallon of milk costs a small fortune. I remember when you could get a whole cart of groceries for twenty dollars. \n\nBut, um... no, I haven't really noticed if the fogginess changes during the day. It just feels like my brain is moving through molasses whenever I try to do my paperwork.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 64 / property_real_estate_or_community_association_manager / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Loss_of_Interest\nmanifestation: \"I have not wanted to go to my dance class, look at the little galleries, or cook Sunday food for my family, and those are things that usually make me feel like myself.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, actually. I haven't wanted to go to my dance class at all lately. Or look at the little art galleries downtown... or even cook Sunday dinner for the family. And those are the things that usually make me feel like myself, you know? 

Sunday dinners used to be such a big production. I'd make a roast, or sometimes a big baked ziti. My grandmother taught me how to make the sauce from scratch. You have to let it simmer for hours, all afternoon, just letting those flavors meld together. She used to grow her own tomatoes in this little patch behind her house. Big, beautiful beefsteak tomatoes. They don't taste like that anymore. The ones at the supermarket are so hard and pale, they look like they've never even seen the sun. It's a shame, really. I tried growing some on my balcony a few years ago, but the squirrels got to them before they even ripened. Those squirrels are so bold! One of them practically came right up to the glass door and stared at me. I think people in the building must be feeding them peanuts, which they really shouldn't do. It just encourages them to nest in the eaves, and then you've got a real mess on your hands for the maintenance crew. 

But no, I just... I haven't had the energy or the desire to do any of those things I usually love.

What brings you in?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor... I guess what brings me in is my head just feels so cloudy lately. I couldn't even figure out what to tell him, and usually I'd just know, snap, just like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Depressed? I don't... No, I wouldn't say I'm unusually worried about anything in particular, just the usual things, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you been forgetting recent things more than usual?\n\nPatient: Forgetting things? No, I don't think my memory is any worse than it usually is. I mean, I still remember all the bylaws for the association, and there are over forty pages of them. \n\nThough, speaking of memory, I was trying to remember the name of that wonderful little bakery that used to be on 5th Street. They made the most incredible lemon bars. My sister Margaret used to buy them every time we had a family picnic. Margaret lives up in Seattle now, it rains so much there. I don't know how she stands it, all that damp. It's terrible for your joints. I sent her a lovely umbrella for Christmas, one of those clear dome ones so you can still see where you're walking. The mail carrier, Dave, he said those are the best kind. Dave is such a nice man, he always makes sure to put my packages right inside the screen door so they don't get wet. He's retiring next year, going to buy an RV and drive to all the national parks. I told him he should start with Yellowstone, I went there when I was a little girl... \n\nOh, dear, I'm wandering again, aren't I? What was the question? Memory? No, my memory is just fine, thank you.\n\nDoctor: What is today’s date?\n\nPatient: Today's date? Oh, let's see, it's... well, it's Tuesday, I know that much because the street sweeper always comes on Tuesdays. They just changed the schedule for that, actually. They used to come on Thursdays, but the city council had a big meeting about it last month. I know because my neighbor, Mr. Henderson—he's the one with that beautiful rose garden, you know, the really big yellow ones that smell like peaches? He went down to city hall to complain because the new sweeper truck is so loud it wakes up his terrier, Barnaby. \n\nBarnaby is a rescue, sweetest little dog, but he has terrible anxiety. Mr. Henderson bought him one of those thunder shirts, you know, the ones that wrap real tight? I don't know if it works, but I told him he should try playing classical music. With the red plug outside? \n\nBut anyway, the date... well, I know it's October. Or is it November already? The leaves on the big oak out front are just starting to turn that pretty rust color. So I suppose I don't know the exact number, but it's Tuesday in the fall.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sick lately?\n\nPatient: Sick? Oh, no, I've been feeling pretty good physically. Just the usual aches and pains, you know. Actually, speaking of being sick, did you hear about the terrible flu going around the elementary school down the street? \n\nMrs. Gable—she's the crossing guard, lovely lady, always wears that bright yellow vest with the reflective stripes—she was telling me that half the third grade was out last week. Her daughter is one of the teachers there. She teaches art, which I think is just so wonderful. Children need a creative outlet, don't you think? I used to love art class when I was a girl. We made these little ashtrays out of clay for Mother's Day. Nobody smokes anymore, of course, which is for the best. My uncle Arthur smoked two packs a day of those unfiltered cigarettes. He had this awful cough, sounded like a tractor trying to start in the winter. He drove a tractor, actually, had a farm out in the valley... grew the most amazing sweet corn. We'd have corn on the cob every Sunday in the summer, with lots of butter. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I haven't caught any of those bugs going around. I make sure to wash my hands very thoroughly, especially after getting the mail. You never know who's been touching the envelopes.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times lately when you felt confused about where you were?\n\nPatient: Confused about where I am? Oh, heavens no. I know exactly where I am, I've got a very good sense of direction. Always have. \n\nMy husband, Richard—bless his heart—he couldn't navigate his way out of a paper bag. We took a trip to Florida back in '98, drove the whole way. We were supposed to be going to Daytona, but he missed the exit and wouldn't stop to ask for directions. Men are like that, aren't they? So stubborn. We ended up at this little diner off the highway, and they had the most fantastic key lime pie. Real key limes, not that bright green artificial stuff you get at the grocery store. The waitress told us her grandmother made it fresh every morning. She was a sweet girl, studying to be a nurse. I always thought nursing was such a noble profession. You have to have a real calling for it, taking care of sick people all day. I used to volunteer at the hospital gift shop, arranging the flowers. People love getting flowers when they're not feeling well. Carnations last the longest, but roses are just so elegant...\n\nWhat were we talking about? Oh, getting lost! No, no, I never get confused about where I am. My internal compass works just fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most days lately?\n\nPatient: Sad? Well, no, not really. I mean, I have my moments, but who doesn't at my age? Mostly I just keep busy. Keeping busy is the secret, you know. \n\nI was just telling my niece that the other day. She just got her real estate license, which I think is wonderful, but she gets so stressed out. I told her, \"Honey, you just have to keep moving.\" When I managed the Oakwood Condominiums—oh, that was years ago now, before they put in that new pool—I never stopped moving. We had 120 units, and there was always something. A leaky faucet, a noisy neighbor... Mrs. Higgins in 4B was always complaining about the people upstairs walking too loud. But they were just normal walkers! They had this beautiful Persian rug, though, to try and muffle the sound. Deep red with gold threading. I always wanted a rug like that, but with my allergies, hardwood floors are just so much easier to keep clean. You just run a dust mop over them and you're done. Have you tried those microfiber ones? They pick up everything. Dog hair, dust bunnies... \n\nBut anyway, no, I wouldn't say I've been feeling sad. Just... busy.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Trouble concentrating? Well, actually, yes. That's a big part of what brings me in. My head just feels so... cloudy lately. I'll sit down and stare at a board packet, or look at a vendor email, and I just cannot decide on even the simple things I used to handle real fast. \n\nIt's so frustrating. Like the other day, I was looking at an email from the new landscaping vendor—we had to switch companies, you know, because the old one kept leaving grass clippings all over the walkways. You just can't have that. People track it right into the lobby, and those lobby carpets cost an absolute fortune to steam clean. We use this company from over in Westville for the carpets, and the young man who comes out, he's just the sweetest thing. Always takes his shoes off at the door. He reminds me so much of my nephew, David. \n\nDavid is so tall, he plays basketball for his high school. He's hoping to get a scholarship somewhere warm, like Arizona or Texas. I went to Arizona once, back in the eighties. The heat is different there, they call it a dry heat, but hot is hot if you ask me. We bought these beautiful turquoise necklaces from a little roadside stand near the canyon. The woman selling them made them all by hand. It must take so much patience to string all those tiny little beads together. I don't have the eyes for that kind of close-up work anymore. I have to wear my reading glasses just to look at the newspaper. \n\nBut yes, my head just gets all foggy when I try to work on the property files. I just sit there staring at the screen.\n\nDoctor: Does this mental fog come and go during the day, or is it about the same all day?\n\nPatient: All day? Oh, well... I'm not really sure, honestly. I haven't paid that close attention to the clock. Sometimes it feels like it's just always there, hovering over me, you know? \n\nLike a thick fog rolling in off the lake. We used to have a cabin up on Lake Winnipesaukee when the kids were little. The fog there in the early mornings was just incredible. You couldn't see ten feet in front of the dock. My husband would take the boys out fishing in that little aluminum rowboat we had. I was always so terrified they'd get run over by one of those big speedboats, but they loved it. Caught some beautiful trout out there. I used to pan-fry them with just a little butter and lemon. You don't need all those fancy seasonings when the fish is fresh. The grocery store fish just doesn't taste the same, does it? It always smells a little off, no matter how much you rinse it. I tried buying salmon last week, but it was just so expensive. Everything is expensive now. A gallon of milk costs a small fortune. I remember when you could get a whole cart of groceries for twenty dollars. \n\nBut, um... no, I haven't really noticed if the fogginess changes during the day. It just feels like my brain is moving through molasses whenever I try to do my paperwork.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. I haven't wanted to go to my dance class at all lately. Or look at the little art galleries downtown... or even cook Sunday dinner for the family. And those are the things that usually make me feel like myself, you know? \n\nSunday dinners used to be such a big production. I'd make a roast, or sometimes a big baked ziti. People love getting flowers when they're not feeling well. Carnations last the longest, but roses are just so elegant...\n\nWhat were we talking about? Oh, getting lost! Sleeping? Oh, I don't know... I haven't really noticed a change, I guess. I just go to bed when I'm tired. 

But you know, the bed I have right now is just awful. It's one of those memory foam things. My daughter talked me into it. She said, "Mom, it'll be so good for your back," because I was complaining about my lower back aching when I'd sit at my desk too long. She bought hers online, it just came in a big box, all rolled up like a burrito. You open the plastic and it just inflates right there in the bedroom. It's the strangest thing to watch. But I swear, it sleeps so hot. I wake up and my nightgown is just sticking to me. I had to buy all new sheets, those cooling bamboo ones they advertise on the television. I saw the commercial when I was watching that game show... oh, what's it called? The one with the wheel. Pat Sajak is retiring, you know. I can't imagine anyone else hosting it. I've watched him since I was in my thirties. Vanna White too, she always wears such beautiful dresses. I wonder what happens to all those dresses after the show. Do you think she gets to keep them? 

But anyway, no, I don't think I'm sleeping any more or less than I usually do. Just the same old routine.

I went in there last month to look for a birthday gift for my niece, Sarah—she just turned 40, can you believe it?—and it was just so depressing. \n\nBut no, this... this trouble deciding things, it didn't start back when he died. I handled everything just fine for years. It's just lately that my brain feels like it's full of cotton wool.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually worried or anxious lately?\n\nPatient: Worried? Well, I don't know if I'd say worried... I mean, I guess I've been a little anxious about the new recycling bins they want to put in at the complex. The city just sent out this huge packet about separating the glass from the plastics, and they want us to use these big blue carts. \n\nNow, I don't know if you've seen these new carts, but they are enormous. I was telling my neighbor, Helen—she's in 2A, right above the boiler room—I said, \"Helen, how are the older folks supposed to drag those things all the way to the curb?\" Helen has terrible arthritis in her knees. She had a replacement back in 2018, Dr. Vance did it over at Memorial. He's a wonderful surgeon, very handsome man, looks a bit like that actor... oh, what's his name? The one who was in that movie with the boat? \n\nAnyway, Helen was saying she might have to hire her grandson to come take the bins out. Her grandson is a lovely boy, goes to the community college, studying to be a dental hygienist. Which I think is a very smart career choice, people always need their teeth cleaned. I go to Dr. Miller myself, he's got an office over by the old post office. The one they closed down last year because of the mold in the ceiling? Such a shame, it was a beautiful historic building. \n\nBut I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, being worried. No, I wouldn't say I'm unusually worried about anything in particular, just the usual things, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you been forgetting recent things more than usual?\n\nPatient: Forgetting things? No, I don't think my memory is any worse than it usually is. I mean, I still remember all the bylaws for the association, and there are over forty pages of them. \n\nThough, speaking of memory, I was trying to remember the name of that wonderful little bakery that used to be on 5th Street. They made the most incredible lemon bars. My sister Margaret used to buy them every time we had a family picnic. Margaret lives up in Seattle now, it rains so much there. I don't know how she stands it, all that damp. It's terrible for your joints. I sent her a lovely umbrella for Christmas, one of those clear dome ones so you can still see where you're walking. The mail carrier, Dave, he said those are the best kind. Dave is such a nice man, he always makes sure to put my packages right inside the screen door so they don't get wet. He's retiring next year, going to buy an RV and drive to all the national parks. I told him he should start with Yellowstone, I went there when I was a little girl... \n\nOh, dear, I'm wandering again, aren't I? What was the question? Memory? No, my memory is just fine, thank you.\n\nDoctor: What is today’s date?\n\nPatient: Today's date? Oh, let's see, it's... well, it's Tuesday, I know that much because the street sweeper always comes on Tuesdays. They just changed the schedule for that, actually. They used to come on Thursdays, but the city council had a big meeting about it last month. I know because my neighbor, Mr. Henderson—he's the one with that beautiful rose garden, you know, the really big yellow ones that smell like peaches? He went down to city hall to complain because the new sweeper truck is so loud it wakes up his terrier, Barnaby. \n\nBarnaby is a rescue, sweetest little dog, but he has terrible anxiety. Mr. Henderson bought him one of those thunder shirts, you know, the ones that wrap real tight? I don't know if it works, but I told him he should try playing classical music. That's what I did for my cat, Mr. Whiskers, God rest his soul. He loved Chopin. Especially the nocturnes. I had this lovely CD player I bought at Circuit City... oh, remember Circuit City? With the red plug outside? \n\nBut anyway, the date... well, I know it's October. Or is it November already? The leaves on the big oak out front are just starting to turn that pretty rust color. So I suppose I don't know the exact number, but it's Tuesday in the fall.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sick lately?\n\nPatient: Sick? Oh, no, I've been feeling pretty good physically. Just the usual aches and pains, you know. Actually, speaking of being sick, did you hear about the terrible flu going around the elementary school down the street? \n\nMrs. Gable—she's the crossing guard, lovely lady, always wears that bright yellow vest with the reflective stripes—she was telling me that half the third grade was out last week. Her daughter is one of the teachers there. She teaches art, which I think is just so wonderful. Children need a creative outlet, don't you think? I used to love art class when I was a girl. We made these little ashtrays out of clay for Mother's Day. Nobody smokes anymore, of course, which is for the best. My uncle Arthur smoked two packs a day of those unfiltered cigarettes. He had this awful cough, sounded like a tractor trying to start in the winter. He drove a tractor, actually, had a farm out in the valley... grew the most amazing sweet corn. We'd have corn on the cob every Sunday in the summer, with lots of butter. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I haven't caught any of those bugs going around. I make sure to wash my hands very thoroughly, especially after getting the mail. You never know who's been touching the envelopes.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times lately when you felt confused about where you were?\n\nPatient: Confused about where I am? Oh, heavens no. I know exactly where I am, I've got a very good sense of direction. Always have. \n\nMy husband, Richard—bless his heart—he couldn't navigate his way out of a paper bag. We took a trip to Florida back in '98, drove the whole way. We were supposed to be going to Daytona, but he missed the exit and wouldn't stop to ask for directions. Men are like that, aren't they? So stubborn. We ended up at this little diner off the highway, and they had the most fantastic key lime pie. Real key limes, not that bright green artificial stuff you get at the grocery store. The waitress told us her grandmother made it fresh every morning. She was a sweet girl, studying to be a nurse. I always thought nursing was such a noble profession. You have to have a real calling for it, taking care of sick people all day. I used to volunteer at the hospital gift shop, arranging the flowers. People love getting flowers when they're not feeling well. Carnations last the longest, but roses are just so elegant...\n\nWhat were we talking about? Oh, getting lost! No, no, I never get confused about where I am. My internal compass works just fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most days lately?\n\nPatient: Sad? Well, no, not really. I mean, I have my moments, but who doesn't at my age? Mostly I just keep busy. Keeping busy is the secret, you know. \n\nI was just telling my niece that the other day. She just got her real estate license, which I think is wonderful, but she gets so stressed out. I told her, \"Honey, you just have to keep moving.\" When I managed the Oakwood Condominiums—oh, that was years ago now, before they put in that new pool—I never stopped moving. We had 120 units, and there was always something. A leaky faucet, a noisy neighbor... Mrs. Higgins in 4B was always complaining about the people upstairs walking too loud. But they were just normal walkers! They had this beautiful Persian rug, though, to try and muffle the sound. Deep red with gold threading. I always wanted a rug like that, but with my allergies, hardwood floors are just so much easier to keep clean. You just run a dust mop over them and you're done. Have you tried those microfiber ones? They pick up everything. Dog hair, dust bunnies... \n\nBut anyway, no, I wouldn't say I've been feeling sad. Just... busy.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Trouble concentrating? Well, actually, yes. That's a big part of what brings me in. My head just feels so... cloudy lately. I'll sit down and stare at a board packet, or look at a vendor email, and I just cannot decide on even the simple things I used to handle real fast. \n\nIt's so frustrating. Like the other day, I was looking at an email from the new landscaping vendor—we had to switch companies, you know, because the old one kept leaving grass clippings all over the walkways. You just can't have that. People track it right into the lobby, and those lobby carpets cost an absolute fortune to steam clean. We use this company from over in Westville for the carpets, and the young man who comes out, he's just the sweetest thing. Always takes his shoes off at the door. He reminds me so much of my nephew, David. \n\nDavid is so tall, he plays basketball for his high school. He's hoping to get a scholarship somewhere warm, like Arizona or Texas. I went to Arizona once, back in the eighties. The heat is different there, they call it a dry heat, but hot is hot if you ask me. We bought these beautiful turquoise necklaces from a little roadside stand near the canyon. The woman selling them made them all by hand. It must take so much patience to string all those tiny little beads together. I don't have the eyes for that kind of close-up work anymore. I have to wear my reading glasses just to look at the newspaper. \n\nBut yes, my head just gets all foggy when I try to work on the property files. I just sit there staring at the screen.\n\nDoctor: Does this mental fog come and go during the day, or is it about the same all day?\n\nPatient: All day? Oh, well... I'm not really sure, honestly. I haven't paid that close attention to the clock. Sometimes it feels like it's just always there, hovering over me, you know? \n\nLike a thick fog rolling in off the lake. We used to have a cabin up on Lake Winnipesaukee when the kids were little. The fog there in the early mornings was just incredible. You couldn't see ten feet in front of the dock. My husband would take the boys out fishing in that little aluminum rowboat we had. I was always so terrified they'd get run over by one of those big speedboats, but they loved it. Caught some beautiful trout out there. I used to pan-fry them with just a little butter and lemon. You don't need all those fancy seasonings when the fish is fresh. The grocery store fish just doesn't taste the same, does it? It always smells a little off, no matter how much you rinse it. I tried buying salmon last week, but it was just so expensive. Everything is expensive now. A gallon of milk costs a small fortune. I remember when you could get a whole cart of groceries for twenty dollars. \n\nBut, um... no, I haven't really noticed if the fogginess changes during the day. It just feels like my brain is moving through molasses whenever I try to do my paperwork.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. I haven't wanted to go to my dance class at all lately. Or look at the little art galleries downtown... or even cook Sunday dinner for the family. And those are the things that usually make me feel like myself, you know? \n\nSunday dinners used to be such a big production. I'd make a roast, or sometimes a big baked ziti. My grandmother taught me how to make the sauce from scratch. You have to let it simmer for hours, all afternoon, just letting those flavors meld together. She used to grow her own tomatoes in this little patch behind her house. Big, beautiful beefsteak tomatoes. They don't taste like that anymore. The ones at the supermarket are so hard and pale, they look like they've never even seen the sun. It's a shame, really. I tried growing some on my balcony a few years ago, but the squirrels got to them before they even ripened. Those squirrels are so bold! One of them practically came right up to the glass door and stared at me. I think people in the building must be feeding them peanuts, which they really shouldn't do. It just encourages them to nest in the eaves, and then you've got a real mess on your hands for the maintenance crew. \n\nBut no, I just... I haven't had the energy or the desire to do any of those things I usually love.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more or less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Sleeping? Oh, I don't know... I haven't really noticed a change, I guess. I just go to bed when I'm tired. \n\nBut you know, the bed I have right now is just awful. It's one of those memory foam things. My daughter talked me into it. She said, \"Mom, it'll be so good for your back,\" because I was complaining about my lower back aching when I'd sit at my desk too long. She bought hers online, it just came in a big box, all rolled up like a burrito. You open the plastic and it just inflates right there in the bedroom. It's the strangest thing to watch. But I swear, it sleeps so hot. I wake up and my nightgown is just sticking to me. I had to buy all new sheets, those cooling bamboo ones they advertise on the television. I saw the commercial when I was watching that game show... oh, what's it called? The one with the wheel. Pat Sajak is retiring, you know. I can't imagine anyone else hosting it. I've watched him since I was in my thirties. Vanna White too, she always wears such beautiful dresses. I wonder what happens to all those dresses after the show. Do you think she gets to keep them? \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't think I'm sleeping any more or less than I usually do. Just the same old routine.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Two weeks? Yes, I'd say it's been just about two weeks. This sharper change in my energy, you know, and my confidence, and then this low, cloudy feeling... it all started right around the time my husband retired. 

He's home all day now. Every single day. I'm used to having the house to myself while I do my community association work. I manage the properties over at Whispering Pines, and it takes a lot of focus to keep all those homeowner complaints straight. But now he's just... there. Putzing around in the kitchen, asking me where the good scissors are. We've kept the good scissors in the same drawer for thirty years! The one to the left of the stove, with the corkscrew and the little corn cob holders. We used to have those little yellow ones shaped like actual corn cobs, but the prongs got all bent. I think one of the boys tried to use them as a screwdriver when they were teenagers. Teenagers will use anything as a tool except an actual tool. My youngest tried to fix his bicycle chain with a butter knife once and ruined my good flatware. It was the set we got for our wedding, real silver plate. You have to polish it twice a year or it gets all tarnished. I use that pink paste, it smells awful but it really does the trick. 

But yes, it's been about two weeks of feeling this way, right since he stopped going into the office.

No, no, I never get confused about where I am. My internal compass works just fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most days lately?\n\nPatient: Sad? Well, no, not really. I mean, I have my moments, but who doesn't at my age? Mostly I just keep busy. Keeping busy is the secret, you know. \n\nI was just telling my niece that the other day. She just got her real estate license, which I think is wonderful, but she gets so stressed out. I told her, \"Honey, you just have to keep moving.\" When I managed the Oakwood Condominiums—oh, that was years ago now, before they put in that new pool—I never stopped moving. We had 120 units, and there was always something. A leaky faucet, a noisy neighbor... Mrs. Higgins in 4B was always complaining about the people upstairs walking too loud. But they were just normal walkers! They had this beautiful Persian rug, though, to try and muffle the sound. Deep red with gold threading. I always wanted a rug like that, but with my allergies, hardwood floors are just so much easier to keep clean. You just run a dust mop over them and you're done. Have you tried those microfiber ones? They pick up everything. Dog hair, dust bunnies... \n\nBut anyway, no, I wouldn't say I've been feeling sad. Just... busy.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Trouble concentrating? Well, actually, yes. That's a big part of what brings me in. My head just feels so... cloudy lately. We use this company from over in Westville for the carpets, and the young man who comes out, he's just the sweetest thing. Always takes his shoes off at the door. I went to Arizona once, back in the eighties. The heat is different there, they call it a dry heat, but hot is hot if you ask me. We bought these beautiful turquoise necklaces from a little roadside stand near the canyon. The woman selling them made them all by hand. It must take so much patience to string all those tiny little beads together. I don't have the eyes for that kind of close-up work anymore. I have to wear my reading glasses just to look at the newspaper. \n\nBut yes, my head just gets all foggy when I try to work on the property files. I just sit there staring at the screen.\n\nDoctor: Does this mental fog come and go during the day, or is it about the same all day?\n\nPatient: All day? Oh, well... I'm not really sure, honestly. My husband would take the boys out fishing in that little aluminum rowboat we had. I was always so terrified they'd get run over by one of those big speedboats, but they loved it. Caught some beautiful trout out there. I used to pan-fry them with just a little butter and lemon. You don't need all those fancy seasonings when the fish is fresh. Everything is expensive now. A gallon of milk costs a small fortune. I remember when you could get a whole cart of groceries for twenty dollars. \n\nBut, um... no, I haven't really noticed if the fogginess changes during the day. My grandmother taught me how to make the sauce from scratch. You have to let it simmer for hours, all afternoon, just letting those flavors meld together. She used to grow her own tomatoes in this little patch behind her house. Big, beautiful beefsteak tomatoes. They don't taste like that anymore. The ones at the supermarket are so hard and pale, they look like they've never even seen the sun. It's a shame, really. I tried growing some on my balcony a few years ago, but the squirrels got to them before they even ripened. Those squirrels are so bold! One of them practically came right up to the glass door and stared at me. I think people in the building must be feeding them peanuts, which they really shouldn't do. She bought hers online, it just came in a big box, all rolled up like a burrito. You open the plastic and it just inflates right there in the bedroom. It's the strangest thing to watch. But I swear, it sleeps so hot. I wake up and my nightgown is just sticking to me. I had to buy all new sheets, those cooling bamboo ones they advertise on the television. I saw the commercial when I was watching that game show... oh, what's it called? The one with the wheel. Pat Sajak is retiring, you know. I can't imagine anyone else hosting it. I've watched him since I was in my thirties. Vanna White too, she always wears such beautiful dresses. I wonder what happens to all those dresses after the show. Do you think she gets to keep them? \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't think I'm sleeping any more or less than I usually do. Just the same old routine.\n\nDoctor: Have these problems been going on nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? Yes, I'd say it's been just about two weeks. My youngest tried to fix his bicycle chain with a butter knife once and ruined my good flatware. It was the set we got for our wedding, real silver plate. Down? Depressed? Well, I don't really know about all that. I just... I haven't really noticed feeling down most of the day. 

It's just this cloudy feeling, like I said. Like when the weather can't make up its mind. We had a week like that last April, just drizzle and gray skies every single day. It ruined my hydrangeas. They were just starting to bud, those beautiful blue ones, you know? You have to put coffee grounds in the soil to keep them that color. If the soil gets too alkaline, they turn pink, and I just can't stand pink flowers in the front yard. It clashes with the brick on the house. We picked out that brick when we built the place in '88. It's called "Autumn Blend." The builder tried to talk us into siding, said it would be cheaper, but my husband insisted on brick. Said it would last a lifetime. And I suppose it has, but the mortar needs pointing now, and trying to get a mason out here is impossible. They never call you back. I left three messages for a fellow named Tony last month, and nothing. People just don't have a work ethic anymore. 

So, no, I wouldn't say I've felt depressed all day. Just... not myself.

It must take so much patience to string all those tiny little beads together. I don't have the eyes for that kind of close-up work anymore. Caught some beautiful trout out there. I used to pan-fry them with just a little butter and lemon. You don't need all those fancy seasonings when the fish is fresh. The grocery store fish just doesn't taste the same, does it? It always smells a little off, no matter how much you rinse it. I tried buying salmon last week, but it was just so expensive. Everything is expensive now. A gallon of milk costs a small fortune. I remember when you could get a whole cart of groceries for twenty dollars. \n\nBut, um... no, I haven't really noticed if the fogginess changes during the day. It just feels like my brain is moving through molasses whenever I try to do my paperwork.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. I haven't wanted to go to my dance class at all lately. Or look at the little art galleries downtown... or even cook Sunday dinner for the family. And those are the things that usually make me feel like myself, you know? \n\nSunday dinners used to be such a big production. I'd make a roast, or sometimes a big baked ziti. My grandmother taught me how to make the sauce from scratch. You have to let it simmer for hours, all afternoon, just letting those flavors meld together. She used to grow her own tomatoes in this little patch behind her house. Big, beautiful beefsteak tomatoes. They don't taste like that anymore. The ones at the supermarket are so hard and pale, they look like they've never even seen the sun. It's a shame, really. I tried growing some on my balcony a few years ago, but the squirrels got to them before they even ripened. Those squirrels are so bold! One of them practically came right up to the glass door and stared at me. I think people in the building must be feeding them peanuts, which they really shouldn't do. It just encourages them to nest in the eaves, and then you've got a real mess on your hands for the maintenance crew. \n\nBut no, I just... I haven't had the energy or the desire to do any of those things I usually love.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more or less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Sleeping? Oh, I don't know... I haven't really noticed a change, I guess. I just go to bed when I'm tired. \n\nBut you know, the bed I have right now is just awful. It's one of those memory foam things. My daughter talked me into it. She said, \"Mom, it'll be so good for your back,\" because I was complaining about my lower back aching when I'd sit at my desk too long. She bought hers online, it just came in a big box, all rolled up like a burrito. You open the plastic and it just inflates right there in the bedroom. It's the strangest thing to watch. The one to the left of the stove, with the corkscrew and the little corn cob holders. We used to have those little yellow ones shaped like actual corn cobs, but the prongs got all bent. I think one of the boys tried to use them as a screwdriver when they were teenagers. Teenagers will use anything as a tool except an actual tool. My youngest tried to fix his bicycle chain with a butter knife once and ruined my good flatware. It was the set we got for our wedding, real silver plate. You have to polish it twice a year or it gets all tarnished. I use that pink paste, it smells awful but it really does the trick. \n\nBut yes, it's been about two weeks of feeling this way, right since he stopped going into the office.\n\nDoctor: During these past two weeks, have you felt down or depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: Down? Depressed? Well, I don't really know about all that. I just... I haven't really noticed feeling down most of the day. \n\nIt's just this cloudy feeling, like I said. It's called \"Autumn Blend.\" The builder tried to talk us into siding, said it would be cheaper, but my husband insisted on brick. Said it would last a lifetime. I left three messages for a fellow named Tony last month, and nothing. People just don't have a work ethic anymore. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I've felt depressed all day. Just... not myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, feeling like myself... it's hard to say. I mean, these stretched out, cloudy periods, these down times, they've actually been coming and going for about a year now. But this latest one, this really noticeable shift, that definitely feels tied to him being home all the time now that he's not working. 

Before he retired, he had his routine. He'd be out the door by 7:15, with his travel mug of coffee. We bought him one of those fancy insulated ones for Father's Day a few years back, the kind that keeps it hot for twelve hours. Though why anyone would want to drink twelve-hour-old coffee is beyond me. It gets that burnt taste, you know? Like when they leave the pot on the burner too long at the diner. We used to go to this little diner on Main Street, Rosie's, every Sunday after church. They had the best blueberry pancakes. Real blueberries, not those little chewy pellets you get in the boxed mixes. Rosie sold the place, though, back in 2019, I think. Some young couple bought it and turned it into a "bistro." They charge seven dollars for a piece of toast with mashed avocado on it! Can you imagine? I buy a whole bag of avocados at the supermarket for that price. You just have to put them in a brown paper bag with a banana if you want them to ripen faster. My mother taught me that trick. She knew all sorts of little kitchen secrets... 

But anyway, yes, the down stretches have been happening on and off for a year, but it's just gotten so much more obvious since he stopped going to work.

She used to grow her own tomatoes in this little patch behind her house. Big, beautiful beefsteak tomatoes. They don't taste like that anymore. The ones at the supermarket are so hard and pale, they look like they've never even seen the sun. It's a shame, really. I tried growing some on my balcony a few years ago, but the squirrels got to them before they even ripened. Those squirrels are so bold! One of them practically came right up to the glass door and stared at me. I think people in the building must be feeding them peanuts, which they really shouldn't do. It just encourages them to nest in the eaves, and then you've got a real mess on your hands for the maintenance crew. \n\nBut no, I just... I haven't had the energy or the desire to do any of those things I usually love.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more or less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Sleeping? Oh, I don't know... I've watched him since I was in my thirties. Vanna White too, she always wears such beautiful dresses. I wonder what happens to all those dresses after the show. Do you think she gets to keep them? \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't think I'm sleeping any more or less than I usually do. Just the same old routine.\n\nDoctor: Have these problems been going on nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? Yes, I'd say it's been just about two weeks. Putzing around in the kitchen, asking me where the good scissors are. We've kept the good scissors in the same drawer for thirty years! The one to the left of the stove, with the corkscrew and the little corn cob holders. We used to have those little yellow ones shaped like actual corn cobs, but the prongs got all bent. I think one of the boys tried to use them as a screwdriver when they were teenagers. Teenagers will use anything as a tool except an actual tool. My youngest tried to fix his bicycle chain with a butter knife once and ruined my good flatware. It was the set we got for our wedding, real silver plate. You have to polish it twice a year or it gets all tarnished. I use that pink paste, it smells awful but it really does the trick. \n\nBut yes, it's been about two weeks of feeling this way, right since he stopped going into the office.\n\nDoctor: During these past two weeks, have you felt down or depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: Down?
