They pick up everything. Dog hair, dust bunnies... \n\nBut anyway, no, I wouldn't say I've been feeling sad. Just... busy.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Trouble concentrating? Well, actually, yes. That's a big part of what brings me in. My head just feels so... cloudy lately. I'll sit down and stare at a board packet, or look at a vendor email, and I just cannot decide on even the simple things I used to handle real fast. \n\nIt's so frustrating. Like the other day, I was looking at an email from the new landscaping vendor—we had to switch companies, you know, because the old one kept leaving grass clippings all over the walkways. You just can't have that. People track it right into the lobby, and those lobby carpets cost an absolute fortune to steam clean. We use this company from over in Westville for the carpets, and the young man who comes out, he's just the sweetest thing. Always takes his shoes off at the door. He reminds me so much of my nephew, David. \n\nDavid is so tall, he plays basketball for his high school. He's hoping to get a scholarship somewhere warm, like Arizona or Texas. I went to Arizona once, back in the eighties. The heat is different there, they call it a dry heat, but hot is hot if you ask me. We bought these beautiful turquoise necklaces from a little roadside stand near the canyon. The woman selling them made them all by hand. It must take so much patience to string all those tiny little beads together. I don't have the eyes for that kind of close-up work anymore. I have to wear my reading glasses just to look at the newspaper. \n\nBut yes, my head just gets all foggy when I try to work on the property files. I just sit there staring at the screen.\n\nDoctor: Does this mental fog come and go during the day, or is it about the same all day?\n\nPatient: All day? Oh, well... I'm not really sure, honestly. I haven't paid that close attention to the clock. Sometimes it feels like it's just always there, hovering over me, you know? \n\nLike a thick fog rolling in off the lake. We used to have a cabin up on Lake Winnipesaukee when the kids were little. The fog there in the early mornings was just incredible. You couldn't see ten feet in front of the dock. My husband would take the boys out fishing in that little aluminum rowboat we had. I was always so terrified they'd get run over by one of those big speedboats, but they loved it. Caught some beautiful trout out there. I used to pan-fry them with just a little butter and lemon. You don't need all those fancy seasonings when the fish is fresh. The grocery store fish just doesn't taste the same, does it? It always smells a little off, no matter how much you rinse it. I tried buying salmon last week, but it was just so expensive. Everything is expensive now. A gallon of milk costs a small fortune. I remember when you could get a whole cart of groceries for twenty dollars. \n\nBut, um... no, I haven't really noticed if the fogginess changes during the day. It just feels like my brain is moving through molasses whenever I try to do my paperwork.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. I haven't wanted to go to my dance class at all lately. Or look at the little art galleries downtown... or even cook Sunday dinner for the family. And those are the things that usually make me feel like myself, you know? \n\nSunday dinners used to be such a big production. I'd make a roast, or sometimes a big baked ziti. My grandmother taught me how to make the sauce from scratch. You have to let it simmer for hours, all afternoon, just letting those flavors meld together. She used to grow her own tomatoes in this little patch behind her house. Big, beautiful beefsteak tomatoes. They don't taste like that anymore. The ones at the supermarket are so hard and pale, they look like they've never even seen the sun. It's a shame, really. I tried growing some on my balcony a few years ago, but the squirrels got to them before they even ripened. Those squirrels are so bold! One of them practically came right up to the glass door and stared at me. I think people in the building must be feeding them peanuts, which they really shouldn't do. It just encourages them to nest in the eaves, and then you've got a real mess on your hands for the maintenance crew. \n\nBut no, I just... I haven't had the energy or the desire to do any of those things I usually love.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more or less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Sleeping? Oh, I don't know... I haven't really noticed a change, I guess. I just go to bed when I'm tired. \n\nBut you know, the bed I have right now is just awful. It's one of those memory foam things. My daughter talked me into it. She said, \"Mom, it'll be so good for your back,\" because I was complaining about my lower back aching when I'd sit at my desk too long. She bought hers online, it just came in a big box, all rolled up like a burrito. You open the plastic and it just inflates right there in the bedroom. It's the strangest thing to watch. But I swear, it sleeps so hot. I wake up and my nightgown is just sticking to me. I had to buy all new sheets, those cooling bamboo ones they advertise on the television. I saw the commercial when I was watching that game show... oh, what's it called? The one with the wheel. Pat Sajak is retiring, you know. I can't imagine anyone else hosting it. I've watched him since I was in my thirties. Vanna White too, she always wears such beautiful dresses. I wonder what happens to all those dresses after the show. Do you think she gets to keep them? \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't think I'm sleeping any more or less than I usually do. Just the same old routine.\n\nDoctor: Have these problems been going on nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? Yes, I'd say it's been just about two weeks. This sharper change in my energy, you know, and my confidence, and then this low, cloudy feeling... it all started right around the time my husband retired. \n\nHe's home all day now. Every single day. I'm used to having the house to myself while I do my community association work. I manage the properties over at Whispering Pines, and it takes a lot of focus to keep all those homeowner complaints straight. But now he's just... there. Putzing around in the kitchen, asking me where the good scissors are. We've kept the good scissors in the same drawer for thirty years! The one to the left of the stove, with the corkscrew and the little corn cob holders. We used to have those little yellow ones shaped like actual corn cobs, but the prongs got all bent. I think one of the boys tried to use them as a screwdriver when they were teenagers. Teenagers will use anything as a tool except an actual tool. My youngest tried to fix his bicycle chain with a butter knife once and ruined my good flatware. It was the set we got for our wedding, real silver plate. You have to polish it twice a year or it gets all tarnished. I use that pink paste, it smells awful but it really does the trick. \n\nBut yes, it's been about two weeks of feeling this way, right since he stopped going into the office.\n\nDoctor: During these past two weeks, have you felt down or depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: Down? Depressed? Well, I don't really know about all that. I just... I haven't really noticed feeling down most of the day. \n\nIt's just this cloudy feeling, like I said. Like when the weather can't make up its mind. We had a week like that last April, just drizzle and gray skies every single day. It ruined my hydrangeas. They were just starting to bud, those beautiful blue ones, you know? You have to put coffee grounds in the soil to keep them that color. If the soil gets too alkaline, they turn pink, and I just can't stand pink flowers in the front yard. It clashes with the brick on the house. We picked out that brick when we built the place in '88. It's called \"Autumn Blend.\" The builder tried to talk us into siding, said it would be cheaper, but my husband insisted on brick. Said it would last a lifetime. And I suppose it has, but the mortar needs pointing now, and trying to get a mason out here is impossible. They never call you back. I left three messages for a fellow named Tony last month, and nothing. People just don't have a work ethic anymore. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I've felt depressed all day. Just... not myself.\n\nDoctor: Before your husband retired, were you feeling like yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, feeling like myself... it's hard to say. I mean, these stretched out, cloudy periods, these down times, they've actually been coming and going for about a year now. But this latest one, this really noticeable shift, that definitely feels tied to him being home all the time now that he's not working. \n\nBefore he retired, he had his routine. He'd be out the door by 7:15, with his travel mug of coffee. We bought him one of those fancy insulated ones for Father's Day a few years back, the kind that keeps it hot for twelve hours. Though why anyone would want to drink twelve-hour-old coffee is beyond me. It gets that burnt taste, you know? Like when they leave the pot on the burner too long at the diner. We used to go to this little diner on Main Street, Rosie's, every Sunday after church. They had the best blueberry pancakes. Real blueberries, not those little chewy pellets you get in the boxed mixes. Rosie sold the place, though, back in 2019, I think. Some young couple bought it and turned it into a \"bistro.\" They charge seven dollars for a piece of toast with mashed avocado on it! Can you imagine? I buy a whole bag of avocados at the supermarket for that price. You just have to put them in a brown paper bag with a banana if you want them to ripen faster. My mother taught me that trick. She knew all sorts of little kitchen secrets... \n\nBut anyway, yes, the down stretches have been happening on and off for a year, but it's just gotten so much more obvious since he stopped going to work.\n\nDoctor: During these down stretches over the past year, have you felt sad or depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: Sad? No, I wouldn't use the word sad. I don't sit around crying or anything like that. I really haven't felt depressed like you see on those television commercials, you know the ones where the woman is just staring out the window looking miserable while the announcer lists all those terrible side effects? \n\nI don't have time to just sit and stare out the window anyway, not with the phones ringing. Even during those cloudy stretches over the last year, I was still fielding calls for the association. Just last October—or maybe it was November, right around Thanksgiving because I remember I was trying to thaw the turkey in the kitchen sink—Mrs. Gable from unit 4B called to complain about the leaves in the gutters. She always calls about the gutters. I told her the landscapers were scheduled for the first week of December, but she just wouldn't hear it. \n\nShe has this yappy little dog, a Pomeranian, I think. Or maybe a Papillon? It has those fluffy ears that look like butterfly wings. Anyway, it barks at absolutely everything. The mailman, the wind, its own shadow. I don't know how her neighbors stand it. If we didn't have a strict grandfather clause in the bylaws, I'd have cited her for the noise years ago. The board tried to update the pet policy back in 2015, but you wouldn't believe the uproar. People treat their pets like actual children nowadays. They push them around in little strollers! I saw a woman at the grocery store with a cat in a stroller just the other week. A cat! And it was wearing a cable-knit sweater. I used to knit sweaters for my grandsons when they were little, beautiful little cardigans with wooden buttons, but knitting one for a cat? The world has just gone completely sideways. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I was sad most of the day. Just... dragging my feet a bit, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period when you felt unusually energetic or up, more than your normal self?\n\nPatient: Energetic? Up? Well, yes, I suppose there were times like that. There were days when I felt almost too bright, you know? Just bold. I'd be at the association meetings and I'd just find myself talking right over people. I just had so much to say and it all seemed so important right then. I remember one meeting, we were discussing the new paint colors for the clubhouse doors, and I just couldn't let Mrs. Gable finish a single sentence. \n\nSpeaking of paint, did you know they don't make oil-based paints like they used to? It's all latex now because of the environment. My uncle Arthur was a painter, commercial mostly, big warehouses and such. He used to say latex paint was just colored water. He had this old Ford truck, a '72 I think, powder blue. He'd load the back up with ladders and drop cloths. He kept it running for almost thirty years. You just can't buy a truck like that anymore. Now they're all computers on wheels. If a sensor goes out, the whole thing just shuts down and you have to pay a mechanic two hundred dollars just to plug it into a machine to tell you what's wrong. It's a racket, that's what it is. \n\nBut yes, I'd get so wound up at those meetings, and then I'd come home and just snap at my husband over the smallest things. Like if he left his slippers in the hallway instead of the closet. Just... snap right at him.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt unusually energetic or bold, did you need less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Less sleep? I don't... I don't really think so. I've always been a pretty regular sleeper, you know? I try to get my seven hours. Though, I will say, it is getting harder to get a decent night's rest on these new mattresses they sell nowadays. \n\nEverything is that memory foam now. We ordered one last year, one of those ones that comes rolled up in a tight little box. Can you believe that? A whole queen-size bed shoved into a cardboard box. The delivery man just left it right on the front stoop. My husband had to drag it up the stairs, and when we cut the plastic off, the thing just puffed up like a giant marshmallow. Smelled terrible, too. Like a chemical factory. We had to leave the bedroom windows open for three days just to air it out. \n\nAnd you know what happens when you leave your windows open in the spring around here—the pollen! Oh, it's just awful. Everything gets coated in this thick yellow dust. You wipe down the patio table and ten minutes later, you can write your name in it again. It completely ruined my outdoor cushions. I had just bought these lovely floral ones from a catalog... they had big pink hibiscus flowers on them, really bright and cheerful. I tried scrubbing them with a little dish soap and warm water, but that yellow stain just sets right into the fabric. \n\nBut anyway... no, I wouldn't say I needed less sleep during those wound-up times. Just my regular amount, really, assuming the mattress wasn't giving me a stiff neck.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt unusually energetic or bold, did you feel more important or capable than usual?\n\nPatient: Capable... well, yes, I suppose I did. I remember I started feeling like I could fix every single building problem in Denver if people would just get out of my way and let me run with my ideas. And that is really not how I usually talk about myself, you know? I'm usually just focused on keeping the peace and following the bylaws. But right then, I just felt so sure I knew best. \n\nLike with the boiler in building C. I was ready to just march down to the basement and re-pipe the whole thing myself! Plumbers charge an absolute arm and a leg these days anyway. We had a fellow come out last spring—Frank, I think his name was. Or maybe Fred? He wore those heavy black work boots that just scuff up the linoleum the second he steps inside. I had just spent three hours on my hands and knees with a Magic Eraser trying to get the scuff marks out of the lobby floor. \n\nMagic Erasers are wonderful little things, aren't they? Though you have to be careful, they'll take the paint right off the baseboards if you rub too hard. My sister-in-law ruined her half-bath doing exactly that. She had this lovely eggshell finish, a sort of pale seafoam green, and she just scrubbed right through to the drywall. She's always been a bit heavy-handed, though. Even when she chops vegetables for a salad, it sounds like she's trying to chop the cutting board completely in half. Thwack, thwack, thwack! I can't stand being in the kitchen with her when she's making her potato salad. She puts celery in it, too, which I just think is a crime against potatoes. \n\nBut yes, I just felt very... capable, like you said. Like I could run the whole city if they'd let me."
  }
